Original: select min(i_item_sk)       ,min(w_warehouse_name)       ,min(d1.d_week_seq)       ,min(cs_item_sk)       ,min(cs_order_number)       ,min(inv_item_sk) from catalog_sales join inventory on (cs_item_sk = inv_item_sk) join warehouse on (w_warehouse_sk=inv_warehouse_sk) join item on (i_item_sk = cs_item_sk) join customer_demographics on (cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd_demo_sk) join household_demographics on (cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd_demo_sk) join date_dim d1 on (cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk) join date_dim d2 on (inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk) join date_dim d3 on (cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk) left outer join promotion on (cs_promo_sk=p_promo_sk) left outer join catalog_returns on (cr_item_sk = cs_item_sk and cr_order_number = cs_order_number) where d1.d_week_seq = d2.d_week_seq   and inv_quantity_on_hand < cs_quantity   and d3.d_date > d1.d_date + interval '3 day'   and hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'   and d1.d_year = 2001   and cd_marital_status = 'U'   and cd_dep_count between 5 and 7   and i_category IN ('Books', 'Home', 'Sports')   and cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 80 AND 100  ;

Let's craft final answer with moderate detail. explain:
- Large cost at Parallel Hash Join d2 (date_dim scan) and Nested Loop inventory index probes. The biggest cost is 139k, mostly from joining d2 and inventory. But data sizes: inventory 133M, catalog_sales 14M. The plan uses index on inventory. The join to inventory on item and date is expensive due to 7-day week expansion. The WHERE inv_quantity_on_hand < cs_quantity filters after index. Could benefit from covering index or partition? But not rewrite.
- Unused left joins can cause unnecessary joins/duplicates; catalog_returns is large (2.1M). Removing them reduces work.
- Redundant min expressions: min(i_item_sk), min(cs_item_sk), min(inv_item_sk) are equal. Repeating min(cs_item_sk) reduces aggregate width and avoids tracking extra columns.
- Predicate pushdown: moving d2 week_seq equality into join and d3 date comparison into join can help planner.
- Potential index recommendations: date_dim(d_year, d_date_sk, d_week_seq, d_date) for d1; date_dim(d_week_seq, d_date_sk) for d2; inventory(inv_item_sk, inv_date_sk, inv_warehouse_sk, inv_quantity_on_hand) covering; catalog_sales(cs_sold_date_sk) INCLUDE (cs_item_sk, cs_quantity, cs_wholesale_cost, cs_order_number, cs_ship_date_sk, cs_bill_cdemo_sk, cs_bill_hdemo_sk). But many columns. Maybe not necessary. We can list as optional with scores.

Need assign rewrite scores. We can define "Rewrite score (1-10, 10=highest impact)". Then table:
1. Remove unused LEFT JOIN promotion and catalog_returns — Score 10 — high impact, especially catalog_returns (2.1M rows) can duplicate rows.

```sql
SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)       AS min_item_sk,
       MIN(w.w_warehouse_name)  AS min_warehouse_name,
       MIN(d1.d_week_seq)       AS min_week_seq,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)       AS min_cs_item_sk,
       MIN(cs.cs_order_number)  AS min_order_number,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)       AS min_inv_item_sk
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN inventory inv
  ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
JOIN warehouse w
  ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
JOIN item i
  ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
JOIN customer_demographics cd
  ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
JOIN household_demographics hd
  ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
JOIN date_dim d1
  ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d2
  ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
 AND d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq
JOIN date_dim d3
  ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
 AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
WHERE inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
  AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
  AND d1.d_year = 2001
  AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'U'
  AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 5 AND 7
  AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home', 'Sports')
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 80 AND 100;
```
</produced_sql>

</advice>
[
        {
            "group": "Join_optimization",
            "produced_suggestion": "Remove unused LEFT OUTER JOINs to promotion and catalog_returns (rewrite score 10). Neither table contributes columns to SELECT or WHERE; the catalog_returns left join can duplicate rows (2.1M-row table), but duplicates cannot change MIN aggregates, so both joins are safely removed."
        },
        {
            "group": "Query_optimization",
            "produced_suggestion": "Collapse redundant aggregate columns (rewrite score 8). Because the joins enforce i_item_sk = cs_item_sk = inv_item_sk on every result row, MIN(i_item_sk), MIN(cs_item_sk), and MIN(inv_item_sk) are identical; compute MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) once and reuse it for all three output columns."
        },
        {
            "group": "Predicate_simplification",
            "produced_suggestion": "Push inner-join predicates into ON clauses (rewrite score 5). d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq and d3.d_date > d1.d_date + interval '3 day' are pure join conditions for inner joins and were moved from WHERE into the respective JOIN ON clauses to help the planner."
        },
        {
            "group": "Query_optimization",
            "produced_suggestion": "No CTE used (score 3). After removing the redundant outer joins, the query is a single aggregation over one filtered join tree; a CTE would not reduce repeated computation and could add materialization overhead."
        },
        {
            "group": "Query_optimization",
            "produced_suggestion": "Optional index support (score 6): date_dim(d_year, d_date_sk, d_week_seq, d_date), date_dim(d_week_seq, d_date_sk) to avoid full d2 scans, and a covering index on inventory(inv_date_sk, inv_item_sk) INCLUDE (inv_warehouse_sk, inv_quantity_on_hand) to reduce heap fetches on the 133M-row inventory table."
        }
]
</advice>

</analysis>
The original query contains two unused LEFT OUTER JOINs (promotion, catalog_returns). Removing them is safe: they do not filter rows, and any duplicate rows they introduce cannot change MIN() aggregate results. The three item-key aggregates are provably identical since the inner joins enforce i_item_sk = cs_item_sk = inv_item_sk, so they are collapsed into repeated MIN(cs.cs_item_sk). The d2 week-sequence equality and d3 ship-date predicate were moved into their JOIN ON clauses, which is semantically equivalent for inner joins and gives the planner better predicate placement. Item join was kept (rather than EXISTS) because item is small (102K rows) and PK-indexed. A CTE was deliberately avoided since there is no repeated subquery computation left to amortize. The result is an equivalent, executable, and more efficient query.
</analysis>

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)       AS min_item_sk,
       MIN(w.w_warehouse_name)  AS min_warehouse_name,
       MIN(d1.d_week_seq)       AS min_week_seq,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)       AS min_cs_item_sk,
       MIN(cs.cs_order_number)  AS min_order_number,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)       AS min_inv_item_sk
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN inventory inv
  ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
JOIN warehouse w
  ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
JOIN item i
  ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
JOIN customer_demographics cd
  ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
JOIN household_demographics hd
  ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
JOIN date_dim d1
  ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d2
  ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
 AND d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq
JOIN date_dim d3
  ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
 AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
WHERE inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
  AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
  AND d1.d_year = 2001
  AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'U'

## Selected Rewritten SQL: SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)       AS min_item_sk,
       MIN(w.w_warehouse_name)  AS min_warehouse_name,

       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)       AS min_cs_item_sk,
       MIN(cs.cs_order_number)  AS min_order_number,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)       AS min_inv_item_sk
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN inventory inv
  ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
JOIN warehouse w
  ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
JOIN item i
  ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
JOIN customer_demographics cd
  ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
JOIN household_demographics hd
  ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
JOIN date_dim d1
  ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d2
  ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
 AND d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq
JOIN date_dim d3
  ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
 AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'

  AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
  AND d1.d_year = 2001

SELECT min_item_sk AS min,
       min_warehouse_name AS min,
       min_week_seq AS min,
       min_item_sk AS min,
       min_order_number AS min,
       min_item_sk AS min
FROM (
    SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_item_sk,
           MIN(w.w_warehouse_name) AS min_warehouse_name,
           MIN(d1.d_week_seq) AS min_week_seq,
           MIN(cs.cs_order_number) AS min_order_number
    FROM catalog_sales cs
    JOIN inventory inv
      ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
    JOIN warehouse w
      ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
    JOIN item i
      ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
    JOIN customer_demographics cd
      ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
    JOIN household_demographics hd
      ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
    JOIN date_dim d1
      ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
    JOIN date_dim d2
      ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
     AND d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq
    JOIN date_dim d3
      ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
     AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
    WHERE inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
      AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
      AND d1.d_year = 2001
      AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'U'
      AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 5 AND 7
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home', 'Sports')
      AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 80 AND 100
) s;

</analysis>
The rewritten query removes the two unused LEFT OUTER JOINs (promotion, catalog_returns) which is safe because the query only aggregates MIN values over columns unaffected by those joins, and potential row duplication cannot alter MIN results. It collapses the three redundant MIN aggregates (i_item_sk / cs_item_sk / inv_item_sk are identical on every joined row) into a single MIN(cs_item_sk) computed in a derived table, then referenced three times in the outer projection. Join predicates are moved into ON clauses for readability. Given the data statistics (inventory ~133M rows, catalog_sales ~14.4M), a CTE was deliberately avoided since there are no repeated subquery computations other than the single aggregate reduction, and a plain derived table is inlined with negligible overhead. Additional index recommendations for the physical layer: a covering index on inventory(inv_date_sk, inv_item_sk) INCLUDE (inv_warehouse_sk, inv_quantity_on_hand) to avoid heap fetches for the quantity/warehouse columns, and indexes on date_dim(d_year, d_date_sk, d_week_seq) and date_dim(d_week_seq, d_date_sk) to accelerate the d1/d2 date joins. The core rewrite improves aggregate work and join graph, and the execution plan topology remains efficient with early filtering through the selective date_dim d1 index scan.
</analysis>

SELECT min_item_sk AS min,
       min_warehouse_name AS min,
       min_week_seq AS min,
       min_item_sk AS min,
       min_order_number AS min,
       min_item_sk AS min
FROM (
    SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_item_sk,
           MIN(w.w_warehouse_name) AS min_warehouse_name,
           MIN(d1.d_week_seq) AS min_week_seq,
           MIN(cs.cs_order_number) AS min_order_number
    FROM catalog_sales cs
    JOIN inventory inv
      ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
    JOIN warehouse w
      ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
    JOIN item i
      ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
    JOIN customer_demographics cd
      ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
    JOIN household_demographics hd
      ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
    JOIN date_dim d1
      ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
    JOIN date_dim d2
      ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
     AND d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq
    JOIN date_dim d3
      ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
     AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
    WHERE inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
      AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
      AND d1.d_year = 2001
      AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'U'
      AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 5 AND 7
      AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home', 'Sports')
      AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 80 AND 100
) s;

The rewritten SQL makes three changes:
1. Removed the LEFT OUTER JOINs to promotion and catalog_returns: neither contributes columns to SELECT or WHERE. promotion's join key p_promo_sk is unique (at most one match), and catalog_returns could duplicate rows, but since only MIN aggregates over columns from other tables are computed, duplication cannot change the results. Removal is aggregate-safe.
2. Moved inner-join predicates (d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq and d3.d_date > d1.d_date + interval '3 day') into ON clauses — semantically identical for inner joins.
3. Collapsed MIN(i_item_sk), MIN(cs_item_sk), MIN(inv_item_sk) into MIN(cs.cs_item_sk). Because of the equality join conditions i_item_sk = cs_item_sk = inv_item_sk, all three MINs are guaranteed identical, so the output values are the same.

All filters are preserved and single-table predicates remain in WHERE. The rewrite is equivalent.
</analysis>

</analysis>
Both rewrites correctly eliminate the LEFT OUTER JOINs to promotion and catalog_returns, which is valid since those tables never appear in the SELECT list or WHERE clause, so the joins have no effect on the result. Both also push the d2 and d3 predicates into the JOIN ON clauses, which can improve optimization. Statement 0 wraps everything in a subquery with an outer projection, adding unnecessary complexity and somewhat confusing aliasing (reusing min_item_sk for multiple output columns). Statement 1 keeps the same simple structure as the original, is fully equivalent in output, and is more readable. Since the aggregation results are identical, the simpler single-level query is preferable.

      "produced_suggestion": "Push single-table filters down to base tables and apply them as early as possible: keep all selective predicates (d1.d_year = 2001, hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000', cd.cd_marital_status = 'U', cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 5 AND 7, i.i_category IN ('Books','Home','Sports'), cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 80 AND 100) as plain WHERE conditions directly on the base tables — do not wrap filtered tables in subqueries or derived tables, as direct predicates let the optimizer use indexes and statistics efficiently. These filters are highly selective (d_year=2001 alone prunes date_dim d1 to ~152 rows), so early evaluation drastically shrinks intermediate join inputs (rewrite score 5)."
    },
    {
      "group": "Constant_folding",
      "produced_suggestion": "No constant folding is needed — all filter predicates already use literal constants (2001, '1001-5000', 'U', 5-7, the category list, wholesale cost 80-100, the fixed INTERVAL '3 day'), so the optimizer can directly use them for predicate pushdown and index/partition pruning. As a defensive measure, check for implicit type conversion: ensure literals like 80/100 match the numeric type of cs.cs_wholesale_cost and that date/interval literals match d_date column types; if a mismatch exists, cast the constant to the exact column data type (or bind a typed variable) so comparisons remain type-safe and index-friendly."
    }
  ]
}
```

**Key merge decisions:**
1. **Join_optimization**: The current SQL already lacks the promotion/catalog_returns joins mentioned in the original suggestion, so the suggestion was reframed as a conditional guidance for query variants while preserving the aggregate-safety rationale.
2. **Subquery_optimization**: The original aggregate-collapse idea (score 8) was fully retained and enriched with the RAG expert's concrete CTE pattern and single-pass aggregation advice.
3. **Query_optimization**: No RAG input existed; original suggestion kept, noting the ON-clause placement is already correct in this SQL.
4. **Predicate_simplification**: Merged the original early-application advice with the RAG expert's additional point about avoiding subquery wrapping of filtered tables.
5. **Constant_folding**: Combined the "no folding needed" conclusion with the RAG expert's implicit-conversion guard, which adds genuinely new value (type mismatch defeating indexes).

Let's set on produce week_seq final answer with strategies, producing many and d2 dates scores, per then conclusion. cs row. Then it joins inventory on inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk and inv_item_sk = cs_item_sk. This is because the optimizer chose to reorder: it first joins d2 to cs on week_seq (which is a non-unique join, expanding), then joins inventory on date and item. But logically, the original join order is catalog_sales -> inventory on item -> date_dim d2 on inv_date_sk and d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq. That join is: for each inventory row, join to d2 on inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk (unique), then check d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq. That does not expand inventory rows. The optimizer's plan reordered by pushing the d2 join before inventory, causing expansion. That might be suboptimal! Let's examine the plan carefully.

Original join order in pre_rewrite_sql:
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN inventory inv ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
JOIN warehouse w ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
JOIN customer_demographics cd ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
JOIN household_demographics hd ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
JOIN date_dim d1 ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d2 ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk AND d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq
JOIN date_dim d3 ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'

The join between inv and d2 is on inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk AND d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq. Since d2 is joined to inv on a unique key, it's essentially a lookup to get d2.d_week_seq for the inventory date, and then filter that it equals d1.d_week_seq. So each inventory row matches at most one d2 row. So the join to d2 does not multiply inventory rows. However, the optimizer's plan shows a Parallel Hash Join between d2 and the cs/d1 set on d_week_seq, then inventory index scan. That is a different join order: it takes the filtered cs rows (with d1), joins to d2 on d_week_seq (which expands), then joins to inventory on inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk and inv_item_sk = cs_item_sk. This expansion is because d2 has many dates with the same week_seq. But wait, the join condition also includes inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk. The

=== Worker 0: Start Rewrite ===
 optimizer moved the d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq condition up as a hash join, leaving inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk as an index condition for inventory. This is a valid reordering: it generates all dates d2 that have the same week_seq as d1, then looks up inventory for each such date and item. This is equivalent because for a given cs row, we need to find inventory rows with inv_item_sk = cs_item_sk and inv_date_sk such that the date's week_seq = d1.d_week_seq. Instead of scanning inventory for the item and checking each inventory date's week_seq, the optimizer generates the set of valid dates for that week_seq and does index lookups on inventory for each date. Which is better depends on the number of inventory rows per item vs the number of dates per week. There are 133M inventory rows, 102k items, 73k dates. If we can force the join order so that inventory is joined before d2, it might be more efficient. How to force in SQL? We can use a subquery to pre-filter inventory with date_dim d2? For example:
SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk), MIN(w.w_warehouse_name), MIN(d1.d_week_seq), MIN(cs.cs_order_number), MIN(cs.cs_item_sk), MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN date_dim d1 ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk AND d1.d_year = 2001
JOIN (
  SELECT inv.inv_item_sk, inv.inv_warehouse_sk, inv.inv_date_sk, inv.inv_quantity_on_hand, d2.d_week_seq
  FROM inventory inv
  JOIN date_dim d2 ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
) invd ON invd.inv_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk AND invd.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq AND invd.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
JOIN warehouse w ON w.w_warehouse_sk = invd.inv_warehouse_sk
...
This subquery materializes all inventory with their week_seq. But inventory is 133M rows, so materializing is terrible. Not good.

Alternatively, use a LATERAL join to force the inventory lookup per cs row:
SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk), MIN(w.w_warehouse_name), MIN(d1.d_week_seq), MIN(cs.cs_order_number), MIN(cs.cs_item_sk), MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN date_dim d1 ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk AND d1.d_year = 2001
JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home', 'Sports')
JOIN customer_demographics cd ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'U' AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 5 AND 7
JOIN household_demographics hd ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
JOIN LATERAL (
  SELECT inv.inv_warehouse_sk, inv.inv_quantity_on_hand
  FROM inventory inv
  JOIN date_dim d2 ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
  WHERE inv.inv_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
    AND d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq
    AND inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
) inv ON true
JOIN warehouse w ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
JOIN date_dim d3 ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
WHERE cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 80 AND 100;

This lateral join might force the subquery to be evaluated per cs row, and inside it, the optimizer can choose to use an index on inventory(inv_item_sk) if available, or use the date_dim d2 join. However, Postgres can still pull up the lateral subquery if it's not complex? Actually LATERAL subqueries are usually not pulled up if they contain joins? It might be evaluated per row. But without an index on inv_item_sk, it might still use inventory_pkey by generating d2 dates? The subquery has inv.inv_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk and d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq. The optimizer within the lateral can use the outer cs.cs_item_sk and d1.d_week_seq as parameters. It might choose to first get d2 dates for d1.d_week_seq, then index scan inventory on (inv_date_sk, inv_item_sk). That's what the original plan did. So lateral might not change the plan. To force inventory scan by item, we need an index on inv_item_sk. Without it, the optimizer's choice might be optimal.

What about the join to date_dim d3? It's an index scan on d3 by d_date_sk, filter d_date > d1.d_date + interval. That's fine.

3. Rewrite SQL:
   Use a subquery or CTE to compute MIN(cs_item_sk) once. Remove left joins. Ensure equivalence.
   Final SQL:
   ```sql
   WITH agg AS (
       SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_item_sk,
              MIN(w.w_warehouse_name) AS min_warehouse_name,
              MIN(d1.d_week_seq) AS min_week_seq,
              MIN(cs.cs_order_number) AS min_order_number
       FROM catalog_sales cs
       JOIN inventory inv
         ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
        AND inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
       JOIN warehouse w
         ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
       JOIN item i
         ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
        AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home', 'Sports')
       JOIN customer_demographics cd
         ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
        AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'U'
        AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 5 AND 7
       JOIN household_demographics hd
         ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
        AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
       JOIN date_dim d1
         ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
        AND d1.d_year = 2001
       JOIN date_dim d2
         ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
        AND d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq
       JOIN date_dim d3
         ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
        AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
       WHERE cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 80 AND 100
   )
   SELECT min_item_sk,
          min_warehouse_name,
          min_week_seq,
          min_item_sk,
          min_order_number,
          min_item_sk
   FROM agg;
   ```
   This computes each MIN once. It removes promotion and catalog_returns. It moves all filters to ON/WHERE for clarity. Note: The original SELECT had min(i_item_sk), min(w_warehouse_name), min(d1.d_week_seq), min(cs_item_sk), min(cs_order_number), min(inv_item_sk). Our output: min_item_sk (for i_item_sk), min_warehouse_name, min_week_seq, min_item_sk (for cs_item_sk), min_order_number, min_item_sk (for inv_item_sk). This is equivalent because i_item_sk = cs_item_sk = inv_item_sk.

   Check equivalence: In original, the left joins to promotion and catalog_returns could potentially filter out rows if there is an outer join? No, left join preserves left rows. But wait, what if there are multiple catalog_returns for a catalog_sales row? The left join duplicates the row. MIN aggregate ignores duplicates. So the MIN values are the same. What if there are no catalog_returns? Left join still produces the row with NULLs. So the set of left rows is the same. So removing them is safe. What about promotion? Same, p_promo_sk is likely unique, so no duplication. Even if not unique, MIN unaffected.

   One subtlety: The original query has `left outer join catalog_returns on (cr_item_sk = cs_item_sk and cr_order_number = cs_order_number)`. Could the presence of catalog_returns affect the MIN of cs_order_number? No, cs_order_number is from catalog_sales, duplicated rows have same cs_order_number. MIN is the same.

   So equivalence holds.

```sql
WITH agg AS (
    SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_item_sk,
           MIN(w.w_warehouse_name) AS min_warehouse_name,
           MIN(d1.d_week_seq) AS min_week_seq,
           MIN(cs.cs_order_number) AS min_order_number
    FROM catalog_sales cs
    JOIN inventory inv
      ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
     AND inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
    JOIN warehouse w
      ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
    JOIN item i
      ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
     AND i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Home', 'Sports')
    JOIN customer_demographics cd
      ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
     AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'U'
     AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 5 AND 7
    JOIN household_demographics hd
      ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
     AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
    JOIN date_dim d1
      ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
     AND d1.d_year = 2001
    JOIN date_dim d2
      ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
     AND d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq
    JOIN date_dim d3
      ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
     AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
    WHERE cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 80 AND 100
)
SELECT min_item_sk,
       min_warehouse_name,
       min_week_seq,
       min_item_sk,
       min_order_number,
       min_item_sk
FROM agg;
```

The original SQL contains two LEFT OUTER JOINs (promotion, catalog_returns) whose columns are never selected or filtered. Since LEFT JOINs preserve all left-side rows and duplicates do not affect MIN aggregates, they are semantically removable — eliminating a potentially expensive join against catalog_returns (2,158,260 rows). Additionally, the join conditions force i_item_sk = cs_item_sk = inv_item_sk, so MIN over these three columns is the same value and is computed once in the CTE, then emitted three times to preserve the original six-column output shape. Filter predicates are attached to their join ON clauses for early evaluation. The provided plan already reflects the removed left joins (cost ~139030); the CTE-based aggregate collapsing yields the same plan shape with marginally less aggregate CPU work.       ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
     AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'U'
     AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 5 AND 7

      ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk

After a careful comparison of the three EXPLAIN outputs, the following observations can be made:

1. The original plan and the rewritten plan are structurally almost identical — the query rewrite primarily introduced table aliases (e.g., `catalog_sales cs`, `household_demographics hd`) and wrapped the aggregation in a `Subquery Scan` node, rather than changing the physical plan shape.

2. The enhanced plan is byte-for-byte equivalent to the rewritten plan in terms of node structure, costs, row estimates, and widths. There is no further plan transformation applied.

3. The only measurable cost differences are:
   - The top-level node changed from `Aggregate` (cost=139030.63..139030.64) to `Subquery Scan on agg` (cost=139030.62..139030.63), a decrease of 0.01 in both startup and total cost — essentially noise.
   - The Aggregate node's output width narrowed from 52 to 44 bytes, and the Gather/Nested Loop widths narrowed from 78 to 70 bytes, indicating the rewrite projected fewer/differently-typed columns (likely due to aliasing/explicit projection), which slightly reduces tuple transmission overhead.
   - The most expensive plan node, the `Parallel Hash Join` at cost 133412.43..135428.01 (driven by the deeply nested index scans over catalog_sales), remained completely unchanged across all three plans.

4. All access paths, join algorithms, parallelism settings, and memory-bearing nodes (Hash, Parallel Hash, Memoize) are identical in all three versions. The rewrite did not trigger any plan-shape changes.

Conclusion: the rewrite/enhancement produced a semantically identical plan with a negligible (~0.000007%) cost reduction, whose practical benefit is limited to readability and a small width reduction rather than performance.

- **Overall cost change percentage:** The total plan cost decreased from 139030.63 to 139030.62 — a reduction of approximately 0.000007%, which is effectively negligible. The plans are cost-equivalent for all practical purposes.
- **Cost change at the most expensive plan node:** The dominant cost driver is the `Parallel Hash Join` node (cost=133412.43..135428.01), which feeds the deeply nested chain of index scans over `catalog_sales`, `customer_demographics`, `item`, and `inventory`. Its cost is identical across the original, rewritten, and enhanced plans — no optimization was realized at the hot spot. The top-level node shifted from a plain `Aggregate` (139030.63..139030.64) to a `Subquery Scan on agg` (139030.62..139030.63), a nominal 0.01 reduction attributable to plan overhead accounting rather than a genuine efficiency gain.

**2. Plan Characteristics**
- **Scan type transitions:** None. All three plans use the same access paths: a Parallel Seq Scan on `date_dim d2`, a Parallel Index Scan on `date_dim d1` (filtered by `d_year = 2001`), and Index Scans on `catalog_sales`, `customer_demographics`, `item`, `inventory`, `warehouse`, and `date_dim d3`. No Seq Scan was converted to an Index Scan (or vice versa).
- **Join algorithm refinements:** None. The join hierarchy is unchanged: a Parallel Hash Join on `d_week_seq` at the top, a Hash Join on household demographics, and Nested Loops for all remaining joins, including the post-Gather join filter on `d3.d_date > d1.d_date + '3 days'`.
- **Elimination of explicit sorting / intermediate result reduction:** No Sort nodes existed in the original plan and none appear in the rewrites. The only intermediate-result improvement is the narrowed row width: the Gather/Nested Loop output width shrank from 78 to 70 bytes, and the Aggregate output width from 52 to 44 bytes, reflecting tighter projections in the rewritten query. The Memoize node on `warehouse` (cache key: `inv_warehouse_sk`) is preserved in all plans, so its cache-based reduction of repeated index lookups is unchanged.

**3. Resource Utilization**
- **Memory usage:** The Hash node over `household_demographics` (estimated 1200 rows) and the Parallel Hash node (estimated 18 rows) are identical in all three plans, so hash-table memory consumption is unchanged. The Memoize cache configuration (logical mode) is also unchanged.
- **Worker count:** All plans plan 1 parallel worker (`Workers Planned: 1`) under the Gather node. No adjustment to the degree of parallelism was made, so the parallel CPU/memory footprint is identical.

**4. Other Improvements**
- **Readability and maintainability:** The rewrite introduced short, unambiguous table aliases (`cs`, `hd`, `cd`, `i`, `inv`, `w`), which improves plan/query legibility without altering semantics.
- **Projection narrowing:** The explicit subquery structure reduced output tuple widths by 8 bytes at the Gather/Nested Loop level and 8 bytes at the Aggregate level, which may yield a marginal reduction in memory and inter-process tuple-transfer overhead in the parallel Gather exchange.
- **Plan stability:** The enhanced plan is identical to the rewritten plan, indicating the enhancement phase did not further perturb the optimizer; the chosen plan is stable and reproducible.
- **Overall assessment:** The rewrite/enhancement delivered a functionally equivalent plan with no measurable performance improvement. The dominant costs (the ~133k-cost Parallel Hash Join subtree driven by range-filtered catalog_sales scans) remain untouched; future tuning effort would be better directed at improving selectivity estimation or indexing for the `cs_wholesale_cost` range filter and the `d_year = 2001` filter, which drive the bulk of the estimated cost.
